\documentclass{article}
\usepackage{ifxetex}
\usepackage{ifluatex}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/488473/78411>
\ifluatex
\directlua{
  luatexbase.add_to_callback("luaotfload.patch_font", function (fontdata)
    local parameters = fontdata.parameters
    if not parameters then return end
    if not (parameters.x_height or parameters[5] or 0) == 0 then return end
    if fontdata.characters and fontdata.characters[120] then
      parameters.x_height = fontdata.characters[120].height
    else
      parameters.x_height = (parameters.ascender or 0)*0.5
    end
  end, "Fix x-height")
}
\fi

% The destlabel option creates "destination names" in the PDF, which allows
% linking to sections in URL fragments when viewing the PDF in a web browser:
% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/65049/78411>
%
% The Makefile previously supported processing the PDF through Ghostscript in
% order to optimize its size. Since TeXLive 2019, this is no longer an effective
% optimization. However, in case processing through Ghostscript is needed for other
% reasons, we retain the pdfa option, which has the side effect of preserving
% hyperlinks through such processing. (An alternative way of doing that would be
% to use -dPrinted=false on the Ghostscript command line.) The resulting document
% will not actually be PDF/A.
%
% To preserve destination names through Ghostscript processing, extractpdfmark
% would need to be used as described at <https://github.com/trueroad/extractpdfmark>.
%
\usepackage[unicode,bookmarksnumbered,bookmarksopen,
            allbordercolors=crossrefblue,citebordercolor=citationpurple,urlbordercolor=linkred,
            pdfa,destlabel]{hyperref}

\usepackage[amsmath,amsthm,thmmarks,hyperref]{ntheorem}

\usepackage[printocg=never,exportocg=never]{ocgx2}

% The Raleway Tabular Lining Figures (TLF) fonts used for pool names (see
% \ralewayitalictype) are vendored under protocol/texmf/, because newer TeX Live
% (e.g. 2025 under `nix develop`) ships raleway without the TLF family or its
% italic Type1 metrics. Register the vendored font map so pdfTeX embeds them.
\ifdefined\pdfmapfile\pdfmapfile{+Raleway.map}\fi
\usepackage{quattrocento}
\usepackage[bb=ams]{mathalfa}
\usepackage[scr]{rsfso}

% Specify how the content should be read out, assuming that the PDF reader supports
% BDC entries as documented in [Adobe2008, section 14.9.4]. For abbreviations that
% should be spelled out, use \readasunicode with U+200B ZERO WIDTH SPACE between characters.
%
% Note that we prioritize screen readers over copy-paste (getting the output right
% for the former does help to preserve the semantic content for the latter).
%
% [Adobe2008] https://opensource.adobe.com/dc-acrobat-sdk-docs/pdfstandards/PDF32000_2008.pdf
\newcommand{\readas}[2]{\BeginAccSupp{method=pdfstringdef,ActualText=\text{#1}}{#2}\EndAccSupp{}}
\newcommand{\readasunicode}[2]{\BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText={#1}}{#2}\EndAccSupp{}}

% Presentation that should not be read out.
\newcommand{\present}[1]{\BeginAccSupp{ActualText=}{#1}\EndAccSupp{}}

% Overlap the same content with a horizontal offset.
\newcommand{\overlap}[2]{\rlap{#2}\present{\hspace{#1}{#2}}}

% Fake bold.
\sodef\spaced{}{0.01em}{0.25em plus 0.1em}{0em}
\newcommand{\fakebold}[1]{\overlap{0.02em}{\overlap{0.01em}{\spaced{#1}}}}

% Quattrocento is beautiful but doesn't have an italic face. So for emphasis, we
% scale New Century Schoolbook italic to fit in with slanted Quattrocento and match
% its x height. (This has the side effect of making all emphasized text into boxes,
% so it won't linebreak, which has pluses and minuses.)
%
% XeTeX and LuaLaTeX require the \fontencoding{T1}:
% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/426300/78411>
%
% New Century Schoolbook italic bold is too wide, and there is no math sans italic
% font, so we override \strongvartype to \strongvaritalictype, and \mathsansvartype
% to \mathsansitalictype to provide better alternatives while in \emph.
\newcommand{\schoolbook}[1]{{\fontencoding{T1}\fontfamily{pnc}\selectfont{#1}}}
\newcommand{\schoolbookitalic}[1]{\scalebox{1.02}[0.999]{\schoolbook{#1}}}
\renewcommand{\emph}[1]{\hspace{0.12em}{\schoolbookitalic{\renewcommand{\mathsansvartype}{\mathsansitalictype}\renewcommand{\strongvartype}{\strongitalictype}\textit{#1}}}\hspace{0.12em}}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/63188/78411> Bruno Le Floch; retrieved 2026-06-15; CC BY-SA 4.0
\newsavebox{\argumentbox}
\newcommand{\slantbox}[2][0.21]{\mbox{\sbox{\argumentbox}{#2}\hskip\wd\argumentbox\pdfsave\pdfsetmatrix{1 0 #1 1}\llap{\usebox{\argumentbox}}\pdfrestore}}

% There is no math italic font available. \textit\textsf has noticeably different
% metrics and glyphs (wider, lower weight, and the g glyph is open, which occurs
% frequently in crossrefs containing "Sapling"). Instead we use a slanted variant of
% real mathsf, which is fine for a sans-serif font in the specific contexts we're
% using it.
\newcommand{\mathsansitalictype}[1]{\kern-0.08em\slantbox{$\mathsf{#1}$}\kern 0.06em}

% Use Raleway Italic for pool names. This is a deliberately idiosyncratic stylistic
% choice (I like the curved crossed staves of the w in Ironwood), consistent with
% the ZIPs as of zips#1301 and zips#1309.
\newcommand{\ralewaytype}[1]{{\fontencoding{T1}\fontfamily{Raleway-TLF}\selectfont{#1}}}
\newcommand{\ralewayitalictype}[1]{{\fontencoding{T1}\fontfamily{Raleway-TLF}\selectfont\textsl{#1}}}

% While we're at it, let's match the tt x height to Quattrocento as well,
% and compress it a little to save space in tables.
\let\oldtexttt\texttt
\let\oldmathtt\mathtt
\renewcommand{\texttt}[1]{\scalebox{0.97}[1.07]{\oldtexttt{#1}}}
\renewcommand{\mathtt}[1]{\scalebox{0.97}[1.07]{$\oldmathtt{#1}$}}

\newcommand{\pagenumfont}{\fontencoding{T1}\fontfamily{pnc}\selectfont\rule[-.2\baselineskip]{0pt}{1.32\baselineskip}}

\setlength{\parskip}{1.5ex plus 2pt minus 2pt}
%\setlength{\parskip}{6.3pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}

\widowpenalties 3 10000 1000 0

\setlength{\bibitemsep}{1.2ex}  % default is too cramped!
\setlength{\biblabelsep}{0.5em} % default is not cramped enough! :-)

\setlist[enumerate]{itemsep=0.3ex,before=\vspace{-1.2ex}}
\setlist[itemize]{itemsep=0.3ex,topsep=0.2ex,before=\vspace{-0.8ex},after=\vspace{1.5ex}}

\newlist{compactitemize}{itemize}{3}
\setlist[compactitemize]{nosep,leftmargin=1.2em,label=$\cdot$}

\newlist{formulae}{itemize}{3}
\setlist[formulae]{itemsep=0.2ex,topsep=0ex,leftmargin=1.5em,label=,after=\vspace{1.5ex}}

\newlist{algorithm}{itemize}{3}
\setlist[algorithm]{itemsep=0ex,topsep=0ex,leftmargin=1.5em,label=,after=\vspace{1.5ex}}

\newlist{lines}{itemize}{3}
\setlist[lines]{itemsep=-0.5ex,topsep=0ex,before=\vspace{1ex},leftmargin=1.6em,label=,after=\vspace{1ex}}

\newlist{poetry}{itemize}{3}
\setlist[poetry]{itemsep=-0.5ex,topsep=0ex,leftmargin=0em,label=}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/15255/78411>
\xspaceaddexceptions{]\}}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/12712/78411> (with X in place of m)
\newcolumntype{L}[1]{>{\raggedright\let\newline\\\arraybackslash\hspace{0pt}}X{#1}}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/112585/78411>
\newcolumntype{R}{>{$}r<{,\,\;$}}
\newcolumntype{S}{>{$}r<{\;$}}
\newcolumntype{T}{>{$}l<{\;$}}
\newcolumntype{U}{>{$}l<{$}}
\newcolumntype{C}{>{$}c<{$}}

\makeatletter
\renewcommand*{\@fnsymbol}[1]{\ensuremath{\ifcase#1\or \dagger\or \ddagger\or \mathsection\or \medlozenge\or \mathparagraph\else\@ctrerr\fi}}
\makeatother
\newcommand{\footnotestar}{\raisebox{0.25ex}{\kern0.04em *}}

\newcommand{\cedilla}[1]{#1\!\!¸\kern0.088em}

% biblatex really doesn't want to support Unicode citation labels, but I will not be beaten.
% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/285834/78411>
\NewDocumentCommand{\citesub}{m}{%
  \saveexpandmode\noexpandarg
  \def\tempstring{#1}%
  \xStrSubstitute{\tempstring}{MAEA2010}{MÁEÁ2010}[\tempstring]%
  \xStrSubstitute{\tempstring}{Hisil2010}{Hı\cedilla{s}ıl2010}[\tempstring]%
  \xStrSubstitute{\tempstring}{Damgard1989}{Damgård1989}[\tempstring]%
  \tempstring
  \restoreexpandmode
}
\newcommand*{\xStrSubstitute}{%
  \expandafter\StrSubstitute\expandafter
}

% Fix the height of citation link underlines.
\newcommand{\linkstrut}{\rule[-0.4ex]{0ex}{\fontcharht\font`X}}
\DeclareFieldFormat{labelalpha}{\linkstrut\smash{\citesub{#1}}}
\DeclareFieldFormat{postnote}{\linkstrut\smash{#1}}
\let\oldcite\cite
\renewcommand{\cite}[2][]{\raisebox{0ex}{\oldcite[{#1}]{#2}}}

\let\oldfootnote\footnote
\renewcommand{\footnote}[1]{\hairspace\oldfootnote{#1}}
\newcommand{\footnotewithlabel}[2]{\hairspace\oldfootnote{\label{#1}{#2}}}

\newcommand{\noborders}[1]{% no nations
  \hypersetup{pdfborderstyle=/W 0}{#1}\hypersetup{pdfborderstyle=/S/U/W 0.7}}

\newcommand{\headingandlabel}[2]{\texorpdfstring{\noborders{\switchocg{labels}{#1}}}{}\hfill%
  \begin{ocg}{Labels}{labels}{false}\fcolorbox{black}{\labelcolor}{%
  \normalfont\normalsize\noborders{\href{\baseurl#2}{\color{black}\raisebox{0.2ex}{\linkstrut}\smash{#2}}}}\end{ocg}}

\newcommand{\refprefix}{\readas{section}{\linkstrut\S\!\!}}
\newcommand{\page}{\readas{page}{p.}\,}
\newcommand{\crossref}[1]{\raisebox{0ex}{\refprefix\autoref{#1}}\hspace{0.25em}\emph{`\nameref*{#1}\kern -0.05em'} on \page\pageref*{#1}}
\newcommand{\shortcrossref}[1]{\raisebox{0ex}{\refprefix\autoref{#1}} on \page\pageref*{#1}}
\newcommand{\theoremref}[1]{\raisebox{0ex}{\hyperref[#1]{Theorem \ref*{#1}\vphantom{,}}} on \page\pageref*{#1}}
\newcommand{\lemmaref}[1]{\raisebox{0ex}{\hyperref[#1]{Lemma \ref*{#1}\vphantom{,}}} on \page\pageref*{#1}}
\newcommand{\footnoteref}[1]{\hairspace\raisebox{0ex}{\cref{#1}}}
\newcommand{\snarkref}[2]{\raisebox{0ex}{\hyperref[#2]{\linkstrut\smash{\textbf{#1}}}}}
\newcommand{\zcashdref}[1]{\href{https://github.com/zcash/zcash/releases/tag/#1}{\softwaretype{zcashd\;#1}}}
\newcommand{\zebraref}[1]{\href{https://github.com/ZcashFoundation/zebra/releases/tag/#1}{\softwaretype{zebra\;#1}}}

\newcommand{\callout}[2]{\vspace{1ex plus 2pt minus 2pt}#1\textbf{#2}\hspace{1em}}

\renewcommand{\appendixautorefname}{ }
\renewcommand{\sectionautorefname}{ }
\renewcommand{\subsectionautorefname}{ }
\renewcommand{\subsubsectionautorefname}{ }
\renewcommand{\paragraphautorefname}{ }
\renewcommand{\subparagraphautorefname}{ }

%\let\oldhref\href
%\renewcommand{\href}[2]{\raisebox{0ex}{\oldhref{#1}{\linkstrut\smash{#2}}}}
%\let\oldurl\url
%\renewcommand{\url}[1]{\href{#1}{\nolinkurl{#1}}}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/60212/78411>
\newcommand{\subsubsubsection}[1]{\paragraph{#1}}
\newcommand{\subsubsubsubsection}[1]{\subparagraph{#1}}
\setcounter{secnumdepth}{4}
\setcounter{tocdepth}{4}

\newcommand{\slightlylarge}{\fontsize{10.5}{10.5}\selectfont}
\newcommand{\notsolarge}{\fontsize{11}{11}\selectfont}
\newcommand{\largeish}{\fontsize{12}{12}\selectfont}
\newcommand{\larger}{\fontsize{13}{13}\selectfont}
\newcommand{\Larger}{\fontsize{16}{16}\selectfont}

\titleformat{\section}{\Large\bfseries}{\thesection}{1em}{\headingandlabel{#1}{\#\sectionlabel}}
\titleformat{\subsection}{\larger\bfseries}{\thesubsection}{1em}{\headingandlabel{#1}{\#\sectionlabel}}
\titleformat{\subsubsection}{\largeish\bfseries}{\thesubsubsection}{1em}{\headingandlabel{#1}{\#\sectionlabel}}
\titleformat{\paragraph}{\notsolarge\bfseries}{\theparagraph}{1em}{\headingandlabel{#1}{\#\sectionlabel}}
\titleformat{\subparagraph}{\slightlylarge\bfseries}{\thesubparagraph}{1em}{\headingandlabel{#1}{\#\sectionlabel}}

\newcommand{\extralabel}[2]{\zsaveposy{before-#1}#2\zsaveposy{after-#1}\vspace{\dimexpr\zposy{after-#1}sp-\zposy{before-#1}sp}\phantomsection\label{#1}\vspace{\dimexpr\zposy{before-#1}sp-\zposy{after-#1}sp}}

\newcommand{\addparttocontents}[1]{\phantomsection\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{\larger{#1}}}

\newcommand{\phantompart}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \addparttocontents{#1}\label{#2}}
\newcommand{\lpart}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \intropart\vspace{20ex}\addparttocontents{#1}\section*{\nh{#1}}\label{#2}}
\newcommand{\lsection}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \section{#1}\label{#2}}
\newcommand{\lsubsection}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \subsection{#1}\label{#2}}
\newcommand{\lsubsubsection}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \subsubsection{#1}\label{#2}}
\newcommand{\lsubsubsubsection}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \subsubsubsection{#1}\label{#2}}
\newcommand{\lsubsubsubsubsection}[2]{\def\sectionlabel{#2} \subsubsubsubsection{#1}\label{#2}}

\newcommand{\introlist}{\needspace{15ex}}
\newcommand{\introsection}{\needspace{35ex}}
\newcommand{\intropart}{\needspace{55ex}}

\newcommand{\theoremlabel}[1]{\def\sectionlabel{#1}}

\theorempreskip{5ex}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/30265/78411>
% <https://stackoverflow.com/a/36706487/393146> for the \phantomsection hack (to link to exactly the right place).
\newtheoremstyle{labelledtheorem}%
  {\item[] \headingandlabel{\normalfont\bfseries ##1\ ##2.\hspace{1em}}{\#\sectionlabel}\phantomsection\label{\sectionlabel}\normalfont\itshape}%
  {\item[] \headingandlabel{\normalfont\bfseries ##1\ ##2.\hspace{1em}\normalfont\itshape ##3.}{\#\sectionlabel}\phantomsection\label{\sectionlabel}\normalfont\itshape}
\theoremstyle{labelledtheorem} % switch to newly defined theorem style

% \cftdotfill{\cftdotsep} matches the dot spacing from the Contents (found in the documentation of tocloft).
\newcommand{\theoremlistentry}[5]{\vspace{0.5ex}\makebox[\textwidth][l]{\hyperlink{#5}{\vphantom{p}\makebox[4.5em][l]{#1}\makebox[3em][l]{#2}#3}\cftdotfill{\cftdotsep} #4}\\}

% We intentionally pass only 4 parameters to \theoremlistentry. The extra one (the target link)
% is effectively "curried". This works around a bug in ntheorem when its \newtheoremlisttype macro
% is used with hyperref.
\newtheoremlisttype{labelledtheorem}%
{\begin{tabular*}{\textwidth}{@{}l}}%
{\theoremlistentry{##1}{##2}{##3}{##4}}%
{\end{tabular*}}
\theoremlisttype{labelledtheorem}

% Theorems and lemmata are numbered together and consecutively within a subsection.
\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}[subsection]
\newtheorem{lemma}[theorem]{Lemma}


\newcommand{\search}{\faSearch}

\mathchardef\mhyphen="2D
\mathchardef\mcolon="3A

\newcommand{\lrarrow}{\texorpdfstring{$\leftrightarrow$}{↔}}

% Using the astral plane character 𝕊 works, but triggers bugs in PDF readers 😛
\newcommand{\rS}{\texorpdfstring{$\ParamS{r}$}{rS}}

\newcommand{\smallcirc}{%
  \begin{picture}(2,2)
    \linethickness{0.4pt}
    \put(1,1){\circle{1.2}}
  \end{picture}}

\makeatletter
\newcommand{\hollowcolon}{\mathpalette\hollow@colon\relax}
\newcommand{\hollow@colon}[2]{
  \mspace{0.7mu}
  \vbox{\hbox{$\m@th#1\smallcirc$}\nointerlineskip\kern.45ex \hbox{$\m@th#1\smallcirc$}\kern-.06ex}
  \mspace{1mu}
}
\makeatother
\newcommand{\typecolon}{\;\hollowcolon\;}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/235120/78411>
\makeatletter
\newcommand*\bigcdot{\mathpalette\bigcdot@{.5}}
\newcommand*\bigcdot@[2]{\mathbin{\vcenter{\hbox{\scalebox{#2}{$\m@th#1\bullet$}}}}}
\makeatother

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/269020/78411>, with explicit size parameter
\makeatletter
\newcommand*{\bigboxplus}[1]{\mathop{\mathpalette\big@boxplus{#1}\relax}\slimits@}
\newcommand*{\bigboxminus}[1]{\mathop{\mathpalette\big@boxminus{#1}\relax}\slimits@}
\newcommand*{\bigdiamondplus}[1]{\mathop{\mathpalette\big@diamondplus{#1}\relax}\slimits@}
\newcommand*{\bigdiamondminus}[1]{\mathop{\mathpalette\big@diamondminus{#1}\relax}\slimits@}
\newcommand*{\bigvartimes}[1]{\mathop{\mathpalette\big@vartimes{#1}\relax}\slimits@}

\newcommand{\big@boxplus}[2]{%
  \vcenter{\m@th\bigbox@thickness{#1}\hbox{%
    \setlength{\unitlength}{#2}%
    \begin{picture}(1,1)
    \polyline(0.1,0.1)(0.9,0.1)(0.9,0.9)(0.1,0.9)(0.1,0.1)(0.5,0.1)
    \polyline(0.5,0.1)(0.5,0.9)
    \polyline(0.1,0.5)(0.9,0.5)
    \end{picture}}}}

\newcommand{\big@boxminus}[2]{%
  \vcenter{\m@th\bigbox@thickness{#1}\hbox{%
    \setlength{\unitlength}{#2}%
    \begin{picture}(1,1)
    \polyline(0.1,0.1)(0.9,0.1)(0.9,0.9)(0.1,0.9)(0.1,0.1)(0.5,0.1)
    \polyline(0.1,0.5)(0.9,0.5)
    \end{picture}}}}

\newcommand{\big@diamondplus}[2]{%
  \vcenter{\m@th\bigbox@thickness{#1}\hbox{%
    \setlength{\unitlength}{#2}%
    \begin{picture}(1,1)
    \polyline(0,0.5)(0.5,0)(1,0.5)(0.5,1)(0,0.5)(0.5,0)
    \polyline(0.5,0)(0.5,1)
    \polyline(0,0.5)(1,0.5)
    \end{picture}}}}

\newcommand{\big@diamondminus}[2]{%
  \vcenter{\m@th\bigbox@thickness{#1}\hbox{%
    \setlength{\unitlength}{#2}%
    \begin{picture}(1,1)
    \polyline(0,0.5)(0.5,0)(1,0.5)(0.5,1)(0,0.5)(0.5,0)
    \polyline(0,0.5)(1,0.5)
    \end{picture}}}}

\newcommand{\big@vartimes}[2]{%
  \vcenter{\m@th\bigbox@thickness{#1}\hbox{%
    \setlength{\unitlength}{#2}%
    \begin{picture}(1,1)
    \polyline(0.2,0.08)(0.8,1)
    \polyline(0.2,1)(0.8,0.08)
    \end{picture}}}}

\newcommand{\bigbox@thickness}[1]{%
  \ifx#1\displaystyle
    \linethickness{0.2ex}%
  \else
    \ifx#1\textstyle
      \linethickness{0.16ex}%
    \else
      \ifx#1\scriptstyle
        \linethickness{0.12ex}%
      \else
        \linethickness{0.1ex}%
      \fi
    \fi
  \fi
}
\makeatother

% We just want one ampersand symbol from boisik.
\DeclareSymbolFont{bskadd}{U}{bskma}{m}{n}
\DeclareFontFamily{U}{bskma}{\skewchar\font130 }
\DeclareFontShape{U}{bskma}{m}{n}{<->bskma10}{}
\DeclareMathSymbol{\binampersand}{\mathbin}{bskadd}{"EE}

% We just want fivedots and medlozenge symbols from MnSymbol.
\DeclareSymbolFont{MnSyC}{U}{MnSymbolC}{m}{n}
\DeclareFontFamily{U}{MnSymbolC}{}
\DeclareFontShape{U}{MnSymbolC}{m}{n}{<->MnSymbolC10}{}
\DeclareMathSymbol{\fivedots}{\mathbin}{MnSyC}{15}
\DeclareMathSymbol{\medlozenge}{\mathord}{MnSyC}{197}

% $v$ is too close to $u$.
% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/questions/130569/sharp-or-angled-v-in-math-mode-varv>
\DeclareSymbolFont{matha}{OML}{txmi}{m}{it}
\DeclareMathSymbol{\varv}{\mathord}{matha}{118}

% These are defined by newtxmath, but that's a very opinionated package that causes a
% bunch of regressions (IMO) to math fonts and rendering.
\DeclareSymbolFont{AMSm}{U}{ntxsym}{m}{n}
\SetSymbolFont{AMSm}{bold}{U}{ntxsym}{b}{n}
\DeclareFontSubstitution{U}{ntxsym}{m}{n}

\DeclareMathSymbol{\Game}{\mathord}{AMSm}{97}
\DeclareMathSymbol{\ggg}{\mathrel}{AMSm}{239}
\DeclareMathSymbol{\circledast}{\mathbin}{AMSm}{254}

% end of characters from newtxmath

\newcommand{\hairspace}{~\!}
\newcommand{\oparen}{\big(}
\newcommand{\hparen}{\phantom{\oparen}}
\newcommand{\cparen}{\big)}
\newcommand{\mhspace}[1]{\mbox{\hspace{#1}}}
\newcommand{\tab}{\hspace{1.8em}}
\newcommand{\blank}{\vspace{-3ex}}

\newcommand{\raisedstrut}{\raisebox{0.3ex}{\strut}}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/415155/78411>
\newcommand{\clasp}[3][0pt]{\stackengine{0pt}{#3}{\kern#1#2}{O}{c}{F}{F}{L}}

\newcommand{\plus}{\hspace{0.01em}+\hspace{0.01em}}
\newcommand{\spv}{\hspace{0.071em}\varv\hspace{0.064em}}
\newcommand{\varvv}{\varv\kern 0.02em\varv}
\newcommand{\yy}{\hspace{0.022em}y\hspace{0.021em}}

\newcommand{\hfrac}[2]{\scalebox{0.8}{$\genfrac{}{}{0.5pt}{0}{#1}{#2}$}}

% <https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/LaTeX/Mathematics#Roots>
\def\hksqrt{\mathpalette\DHLhksqrt}
\def\DHLhksqrt#1#2{%
\setbox0=\hbox{$#1\sqrt{#2\,}$}\dimen0=\ht0
\advance\dimen0-0.2\ht0
\setbox2=\hbox{\vrule height\ht0 depth -\dimen0}%
{\box0\lower0.4pt\box2}}

\newcommand{\possqrt}[1]{\,{}^+\hspace{-0.7em}\hksqrt{#1\vphantom{b}}}
\newcommand{\optsqrt}[1]{\,{}^?\hspace{-0.7em}\hksqrt{#1\vphantom{b}}}

\newcommand{\sbitbox}[2]{\bitbox{#1}{\strut #2}}

  pdfborderstyle={/S/U/W 0.7},

% Typefaces: Presentation

\newcommand{\strongtype}{\textbf}
\newcommand{\strongitalictype}[1]{\fakebold{\textit{#1}}}
\newcommand{\strong}[1]{\strongvartype{#1}\kern0.2em}

% \strongvartype is normally \strongtype, but is overridden to be \strongitalictype while in \emph.
\newcommand{\strongvartype}{\strongtype}

% \indextype is normally normalstyle (plain), but is overridden to be strongitalictype while in \defining.
% This is what makes the page numbers in the index italic bold for defining instances.
\newcommand{\indextype}{normalstyle}
\newcommand{\normalstyle}[1]{#1}
\newcommand{\definingstyle}[1]{\textit{\textbf{#1}}\kern 0.05em}
\newcommand{\defining}[1]{{\renewcommand{\indextype}{definingstyle}#1}}

\newcommand{\mathsanstype}[1]{\ensuremath{\mathsf{#1}}}

% \mathsansvartype is normally mathsanstype, but is overridden to be mathsansitalictype while in \emph.
\newcommand{\mathsansvartype}{\mathsanstype}

\newcommand{\slantedtype}[1]{\textsl{#1}}

% Typefaces: Semantics

% Conformance keywords
\newcommand{\conformancetype}{\strongvartype}

% Network upgrades (Sprout is included)
\newcommand{\upgradetype}{\strongvartype}

% Top-level protocols (Zcash, Bitcoin, etc.)
\newcommand{\topprotocoltype}{\strongvartype}

% Sub-protocols (Sprout, Sapling, Orchard)
\newcommand{\subprotocoltype}{\mathsansvartype}

% Pools (transparent, Sprout, Sapling, Orchard, Ironwood)
\newcommand{\pooltype}{\ralewayitalictype}

% Software packages and libraries
\newcommand{\softwaretype}{\mathsansitalictype}

% Major currency units (ZEC, TAZ)
\newcommand{\majorcurrencyunittype}{\strongvartype}

% Minor currency units (zatoshi)
\newcommand{\minorcurrencyunittype}{\textsf}

% Field names
\newcommand{\fieldnametype}{\texttt}

% Filenames
\newcommand{\filenametype}{\texttt}

% Pairings
\newcommand{\pairingtype}{\mathsansvartype}

% Elliptic curves
\newcommand{\ellipticcurvetype}{\mathsansvartype}

% The arguments are: {anchor_as_formatted_in_text}{index_sort_key}{index_as_formatted}.
%
% \index goes after the term so that the page reference is correct if at the start of a page (see
% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/questions/476644/how-can-i-define-an-new-index-command-that-works-better-for-paragraphs>,
% although our solution is different).
% The method of linking to the index is inspired by <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/399776/78411>.
% \texorpdfstring doesn't actually work here other than to cause an error if we would end up with a
% link in a heading, rather than a hang.
%
% Caveat: if the rendered term (#1) line-breaks across a page boundary, pdfTeX/hyperref emits the
% continuation /Link annotation on the second page with an uninitialised /Rect covering the whole
% MediaBox, causing the entire page to highlight on hover in PDF.js. Workarounds: reword to avoid
% the break at that site, or wrap the specific use in \mbox{...}. Wrapping the \hyperlink here
% in \mbox would fix it globally but at the cost of paragraph fill quality for long terms.
\newcommand{\indexlink}[3]{\texorpdfstring{\hypersetup{pdfborderstyle=/W 0}\hyperlink{index:#2}{#1}%
\hypersetup{pdfborderstyle={/S/U/W 0.7}}\index{#2@{\protect\hypertarget{index:#2}{}\linkstrut\smash{#3}}|\indextype}}{}\xspace}

\newcommand{\codesearchurl}[1]{https://github.com/zcash/zcash/search?q=#1}
\newcommand{\consensuslink}[1]{\fcolorbox{black}{\labelcolor}{%
  \normalfont\normalsize\noborders{\href{\codesearchurl{#1}}{\color{black}\raisebox{0.2ex}{\linkstrut}\smash{{\small \search}\, #1}}}}\,}

% # Terminology

\newcommand{\rawterm}[1]{\textsl{\nh{#1}}\kern 0.05em}
\newcommand{\termnoindex}[1]{\rawterm{#1}\xspace}
\newcommand{\termandindex}[2]{\indexlink{\rawterm{#1}}{#2}{#2}}  % index formatted as sort key
\newcommand{\termandindexx}[2]{\indexlink{#1}{#2}{#1}}  % index formatted as anchor
\newcommand{\term}[1]{\termandindex{#1}{#1}}
\newcommand{\terms}[1]{\termandindex{#1s}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termes}[1]{\termandindex{#1es}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termps}[1]{\termandindex{#1(s)}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termx}[1]{\termandindex{\MakeUppercase #1}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termxs}[1]{\termandindex{\MakeUppercase #1s}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termxes}[1]{\termandindex{\MakeUppercase #1es}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termbf}[1]{\termandindexx{\textbf{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termbfandindex}[2]{\termandindexx{\textbf{#1}}{#2}}
\newcommand{\termsf}[1]{\termandindexx{\textsf{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termsfandindex}[2]{\termandindexx{\textsf{#1}}{#2}}

% Define only the single-argument cases here; others don't help conciseness.
\newcommand{\termconformance}[1]{\termandindexx{\conformancetype{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termtopprotocol}[1]{\termandindexx{\topprotocoltype{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termsubprotocol}[1]{\termandindexx{\subprotocoltype{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termpoolandindex}[2]{\indexlink{\pooltype{#1}}{#2}{#2}}
\newcommand{\termsoftware}[1]{\termandindexx{\softwaretype{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termmajorcurrencyunit}[1]{\termandindexx{\majorcurrencyunittype{#1}}{#1}}
\newcommand{\termminorcurrencyunit}[1]{\termandindexx{\minorcurrencyunittype{#1}}{#1}}

\newcommand{\quotedtermnoindex}[1]{``~\!\!\termnoindex{#1}''}
\newcommand{\quotedtermandindex}[2]{``~\!\!\termandindex{#1}{#2}''}
\newcommand{\quotedterm}[1]{``~\!\!\term{#1}''}
\newcommand{\definingquotedterm}[1]{\defining{\quotedterm{#1}}}

% Use `\readas[unicode]` to resolve ambiguity for listeners ("Sapling upgrade", "Orchard pool", etc.)
% TODO: does `\readas` work in headings?
%
% The `*Text` macros are for headings, and should be used with `{}` for correct spacing.
% FIXME: allow headings to use the semantic macros rather than `*Text{}`.

\newcommand{\Zcash}{\termtopprotocol{Zcash}}
\newcommand{\Zerocash}{\termtopprotocol{Zerocash}}
\newcommand{\ZerocashText}{\topprotocoltype{Zerocash}}

% Network Upgrades
\newcommand{\NUOverwinter}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Overwinter}}{Overwinter (Network Upgrade 0)}}
\newcommand{\NUSapling}{\readas{Sapling upgrade}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Sapling}}{Sapling (Network Upgrade 1)}}\xspace}
\newcommand{\NUSaplingUpgrade}{\readas{Sapling upgrade}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Sapling}}{Sapling (Network Upgrade 1)} upgrade}\xspace}
\newcommand{\NUSaplingNetworkUpgrade}{\readas{Sapling network upgrade}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Sapling}}{Sapling (Network Upgrade 1)} \networkUpgrade}\xspace}
\newcommand{\NUBlossom}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Blossom}}{Blossom (Network Upgrade 2)}}
\newcommand{\NUHeartwood}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Heartwood}}{{Heartwood (Network Upgrade 3)}}}
\newcommand{\NUCanopy}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{Canopy}}{Canopy (Network Upgrade 4)}}
% "N<zwsp>U<zwsp>5"
\newcommand{\NUFive}{\readasunicode{004e200b0055200b0035}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{NU5}}{NU5 (Network Upgrade 5)}}\xspace}
% "N<zwsp>U<zwsp>6"
\newcommand{\NUSix}{\readasunicode{004e200b0055200b0036}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{NU6}}{NU6 (Network Upgrade 6)}}\xspace}
% "N<zwsp>U<zwsp>6.1"
\newcommand{\NUSixOne}{\readasunicode{004e200b0055200b0036002e0031}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{NU6.1}}{NU6.1 (Network Upgrade 6.1)}}\xspace}
% "N<zwsp>U<zwsp>6.2"
\newcommand{\NUSixTwo}{\readasunicode{004e200b0055200b0036002e0032}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{NU6.2}}{NU6.2 (Network Upgrade 6.2)}}\xspace}
% "N<zwsp>U<zwsp>6.3"
\newcommand{\NUSixThree}{\readasunicode{004e200b0055200b0036002e0033}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{NU6.3}}{NU6.3 (Network Upgrade 6.3)}}\xspace}
% "N<zwsp>U<zwsp>7"
\newcommand{\NUSeven}{\readasunicode{004e200b0055200b0037}{\termandindexx{\upgradetype{NU7}}{NU7 (Network Upgrade 7)}}\xspace}

% Protocol-name math identifiers. The protocol analogue of the pool ids (\<P>PoolId); used for
% math superscripts and as the rendering of the protocol terms and headings below, so protocol
% names render as mathsans everywhere.
\newcommand{\SproutProto}{\subprotocoltype{Sprout}}
\newcommand{\SaplingProto}{\subprotocoltype{Sapling}}
\newcommand{\OrchardProto}{\subprotocoltype{Orchard}}

% The Sprout shielded protocol.
\newcommand{\SproutShieldedProtocol}{\termandindex{\SproutProto{} shielded protocol}{Sprout shielded protocol}}
\newcommand{\SproutProtocolAdj}{\termandindex{\SproutProto{}-protocol}{Sprout shielded protocol}}
\newcommand{\Sprout}{\readas{Sprout protocol}{\SproutProto}\xspace}
\newcommand{\SproutText}{\texorpdfstring{\SproutProto}{Sprout}}

% The Sapling shielded protocol
\newcommand{\SaplingShieldedProtocol}{\termandindex{\SaplingProto{} shielded protocol}{Sapling shielded protocol}}
\newcommand{\SaplingProtocolAdj}{\termandindex{\SaplingProto{}-protocol}{Sapling shielded protocol}}
\newcommand{\Sapling}{\readas{Sapling protocol}{\SaplingProto}\xspace}
\newcommand{\SaplingText}{\texorpdfstring{\SaplingProto}{Sapling}}

% The Orchard shielded protocol (used for both Orchard and Ironwood pools)
\newcommand{\OrchardShieldedProtocol}{\termandindex{\OrchardProto{} shielded protocol}{Orchard shielded protocol}}
\newcommand{\OrchardProtocolAdj}{\termandindex{\OrchardProto{}-protocol}{Orchard shielded protocol}}
\newcommand{\Orchard}{\readas{Orchard protocol}{\OrchardProto}\xspace}
\newcommand{\OrchardText}{\texorpdfstring{\OrchardProto}{Orchard}}

% The Sapling or Orchard shielded protocols
\newcommand{\SaplingOrOrchard}{\Sapling{}\nufive{ or \Orchard{}}\xspace}
\newcommand{\SaplingOrOrchardText}{\SaplingText{}\notbeforenufive{ or \OrchardText{}}}
\newcommand{\SaplingAndOrchard}{\Sapling{}\nufive{ and \Orchard{}}\xspace}
\newcommand{\SaplingAndOrchardText}{\SaplingText{}\notbeforenufive{ and \OrchardText{}}}
% TODO: better readas
%\newcommand{\SaplingOrOrchard}{\notnufive{\Sapling}\notbeforenufive{\readas{Sapling or Orchard protocol}{\llSapling{}\nufive{ or \llOrchard{}}}}\xspace}
%\newcommand{\SaplingAndOrchard}{\notnufive{\Sapling}\notbeforenufive{\readas{Sapling and Orchard protocol}{\llSapling{}\nufive{ and \llOrchard{}}}}\xspace}

% The Sprout pool
\newcommand{\SproutPoolId}{\pooltype{SproutPool}}
\newcommand{\SproutChainValuePool}{\termpoolandindex{Sprout chain value pool}{Sprout pool}}
\newcommand{\SproutPool}{\termpoolandindex{Sprout pool}{Sprout pool}}
\newcommand{\SproutPoolAdj}{\termpoolandindex{Sprout-pool}{Sprout pool}}
% There is no Sprout transaction value pool, since JoinSplits are balanced individually.

% The Sapling pool
\newcommand{\SaplingPoolId}{\pooltype{SaplingPool}}
\newcommand{\SaplingChainValuePool}{\termpoolandindex{Sapling chain value pool}{Sapling pool}}
\newcommand{\SaplingPool}{\termpoolandindex{Sapling pool}{Sapling pool}}
\newcommand{\SaplingPoolAdj}{\termpoolandindex{Sapling-pool}{Sapling pool}}

% The Orchard pool
\newcommand{\OrchardPoolId}{\pooltype{OrchardPool}}
\newcommand{\OrchardChainValuePool}{\termpoolandindex{Orchard chain value pool}{Orchard pool}}
\newcommand{\OrchardPool}{\termpoolandindex{Orchard pool}{Orchard pool}}
\newcommand{\OrchardPoolAdj}{\termpoolandindex{Orchard-pool}{Orchard pool}}

% The Ironwood pool
\newcommand{\IronwoodPoolId}{\pooltype{IronwoodPool}}
\newcommand{\IronwoodChainValuePool}{\termpoolandindex{Ironwood chain value pool}{Ironwood pool}}

% Conventions

% requires #1 and #2 to be simple
\newcommand{\pow}[2]{\readas{#1 to the #2}{{#1}^{#2}}}
\newcommand{\bytes}[1]{\underline{\raisebox{-0.3ex}{}\smash{#1}}}
\newcommand{\zeros}[1]{[0]^{#1}}
\newcommand{\zerobytes}[1]{[\hexint{00}]^{#1}}
\newcommand{\ones}[1]{[1]^{#1}}
\newcommand{\bit}{\mathbb{B}}
\newcommand{\byte}{\mathbb{B}\kern -0.1em\raisebox{0.45ex}{\overlap{0.0001em}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\mathbb{Y}$}}}}
\newcommand{\Nat}{\mathbb{N}}
\newcommand{\PosInt}{\mathbb{N}^+}
\newcommand{\Int}{\mathbb{Z}}
\newcommand{\Rat}{\mathbb{Q}}
\newcommand{\GF}[1]{\mathbb{F}_{\!#1}}
\newcommand{\GFstar}[1]{\mathbb{F}^\ast_{#1}}
\newcommand{\typeexp}[2]{{#1}\vphantom{)}^{[{#2}]}}
\newcommand{\bitseq}[1]{\typeexp{\bit}{#1}}
\newcommand{\bitseqs}{\bitseq{\Nat}}
\newcommand{\byteseq}[1]{\typeexp{\byte}{#1}}
\newcommand{\byteseqs}{\byteseq{\Nat}}

\newcommand{\bconcat}{\mathop{\kern 0.05em||}}
\newcommand{\listcomp}[1]{\overlap{0.06em}{\ensuremath{[}}~{#1}~\overlap{0.06em}{\ensuremath{]}}}
\newcommand{\biglistcomp}[1]{\overlap{0.06em}{\ensuremath{\bigg[}}{#1}\overlap{0.06em}{\ensuremath{\bigg]}}}
\newcommand{\fun}[2]{{#1} \mapsto {#2}}

\newcommand{\mean}{\mathsf{mean}}
\newcommand{\median}{\mathsf{median}}

\newcommand{\bigsetof}[1]{\big\{{#1}\big\}}
\newcommand{\powerset}[1]{\raisebox{-0.28ex}{\scalebox{1.25}{$\mathscr{P}$}}\kern -0.2em\big(\strut{#1}\big)}

\newcommand{\range}[2]{\setof{\barerange{#1}{#2}}}
\newcommand{\bigrange}[2]{\bigsetof{\barerange{#1}{#2}}}

\newcommand{\vop}[3]{\,\raisebox{0.29ex}{\scalebox{0.89}{$\smashoperator[r]{#3_{#1}^{#2}}$\,}}}
\newcommand{\sop}[3]{\!\scalebox{0.89}{$\scalebox{1.404}{$\strut#3$}_{#1}^{#2}$}}

\newcommand{\sproduct}[2]{\sop{#1}{#2}{\prod}}

\newcommand{\sxor}[2]{\sop{#1}{#2}{\bigoplus}}

\newcommand{\scombsum}[2]{\sop{#1}{#2}{\bigcombplus}}
\newcommand{\vgrpsum}[2]{\vop{#1}{#2}{\biggergrpplus}}
\newcommand{\sgrpsum}[2]{\sop{#1}{#2}{\biggrpplus}}

\newcommand{\biggercombplus}{\bigdiamondplus{4.6ex}}
\newcommand{\bigcombplus}{\bigdiamondplus{3.3ex}}

\newcommand{\subcombplus}{\bigdiamondplus{1.4ex}}

\newcommand{\combminus}{\bigdiamondminus{1.8ex}\,}
\newcommand{\combneg}{\bigdiamondminus{1.8ex}}

\newcommand{\biggrpplus}{\bigboxplus{3.3ex}}

\newcommand{\subgrpplus}{\bigboxplus{1.4ex}}

\newcommand{\suchthat}{\,\vert\;}
\newcommand{\paramdot}{\bigcdot}

% <https://tex.stackexchange.com/a/87423/78411>

  \docsvlist{#1}
}

\renewcommand{\abstractname}{}
\begin{abstract}
\normalsize \noindent \textbf{Abstract.}
\defining{\Zcash} is a cryptocurrency originally built on \Bitcoin, keeping its transparent
payments and adding privacy-preserving \emph{shielded} payments, which hide the sender,
recipient and amount of transactions that stay within a shielded pool and are proved valid
with zero-knowledge proofs.

\lsection{Introduction}{introduction}
\phantomsection\label{overview}

\Zcash builds on the \Zerocash payment scheme \cite{BCGGMTV2014}, with security fixes and
improvements to performance and functionality, and proves its shielded payments with
\zeroKnowledgeSuccinctNoninteractiveArgumentsOfKnowledge, which over the network's history
have been carried by three shielded protocols: \Sprout, the protocol from launch until the
Sapling upgrade; \Sapling, introduced by the network upgrade of the same name and used for
the Sapling pool; and \Orchard, used for both the Orchard pool and the Ironwood pool.

Value in \Zcash sits in six pools, the transparent, Sprout, Sapling, Orchard, Ironwood and
deferred development fund pools, and transparent value moves essentially as in \Bitcoin,
with addresses and amounts visible on the block chain. Shielded value is carried instead by
notes, each of which specifies an amount and indirectly the shielded payment address the
note was sent to, and notes sent to an address can be spent with the spending key for the
address, yet the note itself never appears on the block chain in readable form, since
anyone reading the chain would then learn the amount and the address.

For each note, the block chain records a note commitment, which works like a sealed
envelope hiding the note while fixing the contents so nobody can change them later. Each
commitment takes a fixed place in a tree of note commitments, and the owner can later prove
a note sits in the tree without opening the envelope.

A commitment alone cannot mark a note as spent, since pointing at the commitment would show
everyone which note was spent, so each note also has a nullifier, which only the owner's
keys can compute and nobody else can connect to the commitment. Spending a note reveals the
nullifier, and every node keeps the nullifiers seen so far and rejects any transaction
revealing one already on the block chain, so a note stays unspent until the nullifier
appears and can never be spent twice.

A transaction can carry transparent inputs and outputs alongside shielded parts, which
spend notes and create new ones, and for each note spent the spender attaches a
zero-knowledge proof showing the commitment is somewhere in the tree, the nullifier belongs
to the same note and the spender holds the right key, without revealing which commitment is
meant. Signatures stop anyone else from altering the transaction, and every transaction
must balance, so no value is created out of nothing.

The recipient learns of a payment from a copy of the note the sender publishes encrypted to
the recipient's address, like a letter only the recipient can open, and the ciphertext
gives no hint of who the letter is for. The recipient's wallet scans the block chain to find
and decrypt the notes addressed to the recipient, and in Sapling, Orchard and Ironwood a
full viewing key sees incoming and outgoing notes without the power to spend, much like
read-only access to a bank account.

With no spend ever naming a commitment, a spent note cannot be linked to the transaction
which created the note, and to an observer the note could be any earlier note the observer
does not control or know to be spent. The guarantee covers fully shielded transactions
only, since the number of inputs and outputs, the timing and any amount moving between pools
remain visible.


\introsection
\lsection{Notation}{notation}

This section sets out the notation used throughout the rest of the specification, grouped
by kind, with each symbol shown alongside its meaning and, where it helps, a short example.

\introlist
\vspace{1ex}
\noindent\textbf{Bits and bytes}\\[0.5ex]
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l@{\hskip 1em}X@{}}
  $\bit$ & A bit: $0$ or $1$. \\
  $\byte$ & A byte: a number from $0$ to $255$. \\
  $\bitseq{\ell}$ & A sequence of $\ell$ bits. \\
  $\byteseq{k}$ & A sequence of $k$ bytes. \\
  $\byteseqs$ & A sequence of bytes of any length.
\end{tabularx}

\introlist
\vspace{1ex}
\noindent\textbf{Writing values}\\[0.5ex]
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l@{\hskip 1em}X@{}}
  $\hexint{1F}$ & A number written in hexadecimal. $\hexint{1F}$ is $31$. \\
  $\ascii{abc}$ & Text stored as \xUSASCII bytes. $\ascii{abc}$ is the bytes $\hexarray{61,62,63}$. \\
  $\zerobytes{\ell}$ & $\ell$ zero bytes in a row. \\
  $\zeros{\ell}$, $\ones{\ell}$ & $\ell$ zero bits, or $\ell$ one bits, in a row.
\end{tabularx}

\introlist
\vspace{1ex}
\noindent\textbf{Sequences}\\[0.5ex]
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l@{\hskip 1em}X@{}}
  $a \bconcat b$ & $a$ followed by $b$, joined into one sequence. \\
  $\length(S)$ & How many elements $S$ has. \\
  $\truncate{k}(S)$ & The first $k$ elements of $S$. \\
  $\range{a}{b}$ & Every integer from $a$ to $b$, including both ends. \\
  $x_{1..N}$ & Elements $1$ to $N$ of $x$. Counting starts at $1$, not $0$.
\end{tabularx}

\introlist
\vspace{1ex}
\noindent\textbf{Arithmetic}\\[0.5ex]
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l@{\hskip 1em}X@{}}
  $a \xor b$ & Bitwise XOR of $a$ and $b$. \\
  $a \band b$ & Bitwise AND of $a$ and $b$. \\
  $\floor{x}$, $\ceiling{x}$ & $x$ rounded down; $x$ rounded up. \\
  $a \bmod q$ & The remainder when $a$ is divided by $q$. \\
  $b \bchoose x : y$ & $x$ if $b$ is $1$; $y$ if $b$ is $0$.
\end{tabularx}

\introlist
\vspace{1ex}
\noindent\textbf{Special values}\\[0.5ex]
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l@{\hskip 1em}X@{}}
  $\bot$ & Failure: a check or decryption failed, or the value is not available.
\end{tabularx}

\introlist
\vspace{1ex}
\noindent\textbf{Used in the cryptography sections}\\[0.5ex]
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}l@{\hskip 1em}X@{}}
  $k \pmod{q}$ & The integer $k$, treated as an element of the field with $q$ elements. \\
  $\possqrt{a}$ & The square root of $a$ that lies between $0$ and $\frac{q-1}{2}$.
                  Only used where a square root exists. \\
  $\optsqrt{a}$ & Either square root of $a$, or $\bot$ if $a$ has none. \\
  \defining{\xCtEdwards} & Short for \completeTwistedEdwardsEllipticCurves and coordinates.
                  See \crossref{jubjub}.
\end{tabularx}


\pagebreak
\lsection{Concepts}{concepts}
\phantomsection\label{addressesandkeys}\label{notes}\label{noteptconcept}\label{notecommitmentconcept}%
\label{nullifierconcept}\label{blockchain}\label{transactions}\label{joinsplit}\label{spendsandoutputs}%
\label{actions}\label{networks}

\textbf{Keys and addresses.} To receive a shielded payment, a user needs a shielded payment
address, which a wallet makes from a spending key together with a chain of weaker keys, each able
to make the keys below it but never the keys above. A full viewing key shows incoming and outgoing
payments, an incoming viewing key shows only incoming payments, and an address lets others pay the
user while showing nothing. Most wallets make \Sapling and \Orchard keys from one seed, as
\cite{ZIP-32} describes.

Payments to one address do not show on the block chain as payments to one user, not even to the
payers, but two payers who compare addresses can see the match. \Sprout gives one address to each
spending key, while \Sapling and \Orchard let one spending key make many rotating (diversified)
addresses, which share the same viewing keys, so a user can give each payer a different address and
scan the block chain once for all of them.

\textbf{Notes.} A note belongs to one shielded pool and records an amount in zatoshi, the address
the note was sent to, and random values for the note commitment and the nullifier. Each note goes
onto the block chain encrypted to the recipient, with a 512-byte memo for the sender and the
recipient to use as they agree, and each encrypted note starts with a version byte whose allowed
values depend on the pool and the block height.

From block 3,363,426 on Mainnet until the NU6.2 upgrade, no transaction could contain \Orchard
Actions. Since NU6.3, value can leave the Orchard pool but cannot go into the Orchard pool, and new
payments to \Orchard addresses go into the Ironwood pool, which uses the \Orchard protocol.

\textbf{Commitments and nullifiers.} When a transaction makes a note, the note commitment goes into
the note commitment tree of the note's pool, and when a transaction spends the note, a
zero-knowledge proof shows the note commitment is in the tree without showing which note
commitment belongs to the note. Spending the note puts the note's nullifier into the pool's
nullifier set, and a transaction is not valid if the nullifier is already in the set, so each note
can be spent only once. The nullifier comes from values in the note and from a key of the owner,
the spending key in \Sprout and the nullifier deriving key in \Sapling, \Orchard and Ironwood.

\textbf{The block chain.} Each node sees a tree of candidate blocks that starts at the genesis
block, and a path of valid blocks through the tree is a valid block chain, with block heights from
$0$ that nodes must support up to $2^{31} - 1$. A node follows the valid block chain with the most
total work, takes the block received first when two blocks tie, and may refuse to roll back more
than a fixed number of blocks, $100$ for zcashd and zebra.

A network upgrade is settled when there is social consensus on the block where the upgrade
activated, and software that holds Mainnet funds must follow a block chain that contains that block
for the most recent settled upgrade. Social consensus also decides which node software follows the
Mainnet rules, at present zebra from the Zcash Foundation and zcashd from the Zcash Open
Development Lab, and zcashd will not support the changes in NU6.3.

\textbf{Transactions and treestates.} Each transaction has a transaction ID, which block explorers,
other transactions and the block's Merkle tree use to refer to the transaction. In a transaction,
transparent inputs and shielded spends add value to a balance, transparent outputs and shielded
outputs take value from the balance, and the value that remains, which must not be negative, is
the fee for the miner.

Each shielded pool has a treestate, made of the note commitment tree and the nullifier set, which
passes from each transaction to the next transaction and from each block to the next block. A spend
refers to an anchor, the root of the note commitment tree at an earlier block, and proves the note
commitment is in the tree at that point.

\textbf{Shielded transfers.} A \Sprout JoinSplit spends up to two notes and makes up to two notes,
proves inside the proof that the values balance, and can use the result of an earlier JoinSplit in
the same transaction. \Sapling uses a separate Spend for each spent note and a separate Output for
each new note, each with a value commitment, and one binding signature proves the value commitments
of the whole transaction balance. \Orchard puts one optional spend and one optional output into
each Action, with one value commitment for the net value, and puts the proofs for all Actions of a
transaction together, and neither \Sapling nor \Orchard lets a transaction spend a note made in the
same transaction.

\textbf{Trees and nullifier sets.} The note commitment tree of each pool is a Merkle tree of fixed
depth that only grows, which shows value exists in the way the set of unspent outputs does in
\Bitcoin but does not prevent double spending, and no block may add more note commitments than the
tree can hold. Each node keeps one nullifier set for each pool, no nullifier may appear twice in a
valid block chain, and the nullifier sets of different pools stay separate even when two
nullifiers have the same bits.

\textbf{Block rewards.} Like \Bitcoin, \Zcash makes new currency with each block, and the block
subsidy goes to the miner and, for the first four years, to the Founders' Reward, or after that to
the developer funding streams. The coinbase transaction, the first transaction in each block,
collects the block subsidy and the fees of the other transactions, and chapter 7 gives the amounts.

\textbf{Testnet.} Testnet is a public test network next to Mainnet, with a TAZ token that has no
intended value, and Testnet usually activates each network upgrade before Mainnet to find errors in
the specification or the software, but the Testnet block chain can be rolled back at any time. On
both networks the smallest unit of currency is the zatoshi, and one ZEC or one TAZ is $10^8$
zatoshi.


\introsection
\extralabel{cautionlinkage}{\lsection{Abstract Protocol}{abstractprotocol}}

\lsubsection{Cryptographic tools}{readabletools}
\phantomsection\label{abstractcommit}\label{abstractgroup}\label{abstractkdf}\label{abstractkeyagreement}\label{abstractprfs}\label{abstractprps}\label{abstractsig}\label{abstractsigmono}\label{abstractsigrerand}%

A public ledger lets every node check every payment by showing everything, and a shielded payment has to hide the same facts without losing the check. Zcash manages the trade with a small set of cryptographic tools, each described here by the guarantee the tool gives rather than by the algorithm behind the guarantee.

\textbf{Commitments.} A note has to go onto the block chain without being readable, and a commitment works like a sealed envelope, hiding the note while fixing the contents so nobody can change them later.

\textbf{Pseudo-random functions.} A spent note then needs a mark only the owner can compute and nobody can connect to the envelope. A pseudo-random function, a keyed hash whose outputs look random to anyone without the key, gives exactly such a mark, and a note's nullifier is one of these outputs under a key belonging to the owner.

\textbf{Zero-knowledge proofs.} The owner must still convince every node the hidden note exists and belongs to the owner. A zero-knowledge proof shows secret values pass a public list of checks while revealing nothing else about the values, and a correct proof always verifies, while nobody can feasibly prove a false claim. Sprout proofs use BCTV14 and later Groth16, Sapling proofs use Groth16, and Orchard and Ironwood proofs use Halo 2.

\textbf{Signatures.} A proof on the block chain could be copied into another transaction, or altered by someone without the secret values into a proof about related values, and signatures over the whole transaction prevent both. Zcash uses two unusual kinds of signature, one whose key can be re-randomized for each spend and one whose keys can be added together.

\textbf{Value commitments.} Amounts are hidden too, yet a node must still check the value going into a transaction matches the value coming out. The value commitments of Sapling and Orchard can be added together without being opened, and a signature whose keys add in the same way shows the hidden amounts balance.

\textbf{Encryption.} The recipient has to learn what the note contains while nobody else does. Key agreement gives the sender and the recipient one shared secret, a key derivation function turns the secret into a key, and authenticated encryption seals the note, hiding both the contents and the address the note is for.

\textbf{Hash functions.} Nobody can feasibly find two inputs to a hash function with the same output, and hash functions build the tree of note commitments and give the fixed curve points behind commitments and addresses.

\lsubsection{Keys}{readablekeys}
\phantomsection\label{orchardkeycomponents}\label{saplingkeycomponents}\label{sproutkeycomponents}%

A wallet with one key for everything would put the power to spend at risk every time the wallet only needed to read or receive a payment. Zcash instead derives a chain of keys from one random spending key, and each step runs one way, so a weaker key can be handed out without exposing the keys above.

\textbf{Sapling.} The spending key gives three secrets, the spend authorizing key for approving spends, the nullifier private key for computing nullifiers and the outgoing viewing key for reading payments sent. The public halves $\mathit{ak}$ and $\mathit{nk}$ of the first two, together with the outgoing viewing key, form the full viewing key, and a hash of $\mathit{ak}$ and $\mathit{nk}$ gives the incoming viewing key, which finds and reads payments received.

\textbf{Orchard and Ironwood.} The keys keep the same roles, but the outgoing viewing key and a diversifier key come from the full viewing key rather than straight from the spending key. Wallets must derive Orchard keys exactly as specified, or the notes may not be recoverable through the recovery protocol of \cite{ZIP-2005} if attacks with quantum computers become practical.

\textbf{Sprout.} The spending key gives one paying key and one receiving key, and each spending key has exactly one address.

\textbf{Addresses.} A Sapling or Orchard address pairs a diversifier with a transmission key made from the diversifier and the incoming viewing key, so every new diversifier gives a new address. The addresses work like post-office boxes forwarding to one mailroom, where outsiders cannot tell which boxes belong together while the one incoming viewing key finds the mail sent to every box. Orchard makes each diversifier from a diversifier index, and wallets should not pick the index at random \cite{ZIP-32}.

\textbf{Failed derivations.} A derivation can now and then produce a value which is not a valid key, and the wallet then discards the spending key and starts again with a new one.

\lsubsection{Shielded parts of a transaction}{readabledescriptions}
\phantomsection\label{joinsplitdesc}\label{outputdesc}\label{spenddesc}%

Nodes cannot see a shielded transfer, so the transaction carries a description of the transfer instead, holding the public values the checks need and a proof the hidden values behind them are right. No description shows a note or a shielded address, every field must be a valid encoding, and every proof must verify.

\textbf{JoinSplits.} A Sprout JoinSplit spends two notes and creates two, and two public amounts move value in or out of the transparent side of the transaction, at most one of them non-zero. Since Canopy the amount moving into Sprout must be zero, which closes the Sprout pool to new value.

\textbf{Spends and Outputs.} Sapling gives each spent note a Spend, which carries a spend authorization signature over the whole transaction, and each new note an Output, which carries the encrypted note and no signature. The value commitment and the randomized key of a Spend, and the value commitment and the ephemeral key of an Output, must not be points of small order.

\textbf{Actions.} Orchard and Ironwood join one optional spend and one optional output into each Action, with one value commitment for the net value. Two flags for each pool say whether the Actions may spend and may create non-zero value, and from NU6.3 a third flag says whether a new note may go to a different address from the spent note. Every field of an Action must be a canonical encoding, and the randomized key must not be the identity point. The proof must verify under the verifying key of the circuit in force, and the circuit changed at NU6.2, to fix a flaw which could have let a transaction break the balance rule, and again at NU6.3.

\lsubsection{Sending notes and dummy notes}{readablesending}
\phantomsection\label{sproutdummynotes}%

A sender has the recipient's address and no private channel to the recipient, so the sender builds the whole note alone and puts everything the recipient needs on the block chain. The sender chooses fresh random values, computes the value commitment and the note commitment, encrypts the note to the recipient and makes the proof.

Since Canopy most of a note's random values come from one 32-byte seed, $\mathit{rseed}$, which travels inside the encrypted note so the recipient can rebuild the note exactly. In Orchard the new note's $\rho$ is the nullifier of the note spent in the same Action. The sender also adds a second, shorter ciphertext readable by an outgoing viewing key, which lets the sender read the payment again later, or readable by no key at all, which keeps the payment secret even if the sender's keys later leak.

Wallets should shuffle the order of spends and outputs to reveal less about the transaction. Since Canopy wallets must not send to Sprout addresses \cite{ZIP-211}.

\textbf{Dummy notes.} A transaction can still give away how many notes the sender really spends, and dummy notes hide the count. A dummy note is a note of zero value under a throwaway key, like a cheque made out for nothing, which fills a place in a transaction without moving money. JoinSplits use dummies to fill the fixed room for two notes in and two out, and Sapling and Orchard use dummy spends to hide the number of real spends. An Orchard Action with no real note to spend should spend a dummy note and take the dummy's nullifier as the new note's $\rho$. A dummy passes every check a real note passes except one, as the proof does not require a note of zero value to be in the tree.

\lsubsection{Merkle paths}{readablemerkle}

A spender has to show the spent note is one of the notes on the block chain without saying which one. Each pool keeps the pool's note commitments in a Merkle tree of fixed depth which only grows. A Merkle path lists the neighbouring hash at each level from one note commitment up to the root, so anyone holding the commitment and the path can recompute the root. The spender keeps the commitment and the path secret inside the proof, and the transaction names only the root, called the anchor, so nodes learn the note is in the tree but not where.

\lsubsection{Signatures and balance}{readablesigbalance}
\phantomsection\label{orchardbalance}\label{saplingbalance}\label{sighash}\label{spendauthsig}\label{sproutnonmalleability}%

Once the parts are built, nobody should be able to change the transaction, and every node must be able to check the hidden amounts balance. Each signature in a shielded transaction signs the SIGHASH, a hash of the whole transaction, computed for version 4 as \cite{ZIP-243} specifies, for version 5 as \cite{ZIP-244} specifies and for version 6 as \cite{ZIP-229} specifies. From NU5 the SIGHASH type must be one of the defined types in canonical form, and every transaction must name the consensus branch of the upgrade in force.

\textbf{Sprout.} Sprout signs each transaction with a throwaway key used once, and the proof ties the key to the owners of the spent notes through the value $h_{\mathsf{Sig}}$. Sprout also checks the balance inside the proof.

\textbf{Balance.} Sapling and Orchard check the balance outside the proof, with value commitments which can be added together. A commitment to a value $v$ with a random trapdoor $\mathit{rcv}$ is

\begin{center}$\mathit{cv} = v \cdot V + \mathit{rcv} \cdot R$\end{center}

for two fixed points V and R, so adding commitments adds the values and the trapdoors separately. A node adds the commitments of the spends, subtracts those of the outputs and subtracts the public amount the pool releases to the transparent side. In an honest transaction the values cancel and only a multiple of R remains, a public key whose private key, the sum of the trapdoors, only the builder of the transaction knows.

The builder signs the transaction with the private key, and the signature, called the binding signature, must verify under the public key each node computes from the commitments. If the values did not cancel, a valid signature would need one commitment opened to two different values, which the commitments rule out. Every value fits in 64 bits and a transaction holds only so many parts, so the sum cannot wrap round to zero either. Proving knowledge of the trapdoors also stops anyone copying an Output into another transaction, and Orchard and Ironwood work the same way, with one net value commitment per Action and one binding signature per pool.

\textbf{Spend authorization.} The binding signature shows the values balance but not the spender's right to spend them, and a separate spend authorization signature proves the spender holds the key of each spent note. Keeping the signature outside the proof lets a device with little memory or computing power, such as a hardware wallet, approve a spend without building the proof. A signature checked against the owner's own key would link every spend to the owner's address. The wallet instead shifts the key by a fresh random value for each spend and publishes the shifted key $\mathit{rk}$, the owner's key in a fresh disguise, and the proof shows $\mathit{rk}$ disguises the right key. A wallet may hand the proof to a helper, who then learns enough to see the owner's payments but still cannot spend.

\lsubsection{Nullifiers and pool balances}{readablenullifiers}
\phantomsection\label{rhoandnullifiers}%

A nullifier has to follow from the note alone, differ from the nullifier of every other note, and stay unlinkable to the note for anyone without the owner's key, even the sender. Each note therefore has a value $\rho$ no other note shares, which Sprout and Orchard notes carry inside the note and Sapling computes from the note commitment and the note's place in the tree. The nullifier is computed from $\rho$ under a key of the owner, the spending key in Sprout and the nullifier deriving key in Sapling and Orchard.

\textbf{Faerie Gold.} Without unique $\rho$ values a payer could send two notes with the same nullifier, both of which look valid to the recipient although only one can ever be spent, an attack named Faerie Gold. Orchard rules the attack out by setting each new note's $\rho$ to the nullifier of the note spent in the same Action, and since each nullifier appears on the chain at most once, every $\rho$ is unique.

\textbf{Pool balances.} Nullifiers protect each note, and pool balances protect the supply as a whole. Each chain value pool keeps a running balance of the value the pool has received minus the value paid out, and a block which would make any pool's balance negative is invalid. The rule works like a turnstile at the entrance of each pool, so a bug in a shielded pool could create false value inside the pool, but no more value could leave the pool than entered \cite{ZIP-209}. The total supply across all pools must never exceed 21 million ZEC, and from NU7 the balance the Network Sustainability Mechanism holds for reissue must also stay non-negative.

\lsubsection{What the proofs prove}{readableproofs}
\phantomsection\label{spendstatement}\label{sproutnullifierintegrity}\label{sproutspendauthority}%

Each proof has public inputs, which appear in the transaction, and a witness, which stays with the prover, and a node verifying the proof learns nothing beyond the checks below passing.

A spend proof shows the spent note's commitment is in the tree under the anchor, the nullifier is the right one for the note, and the randomized key $\mathit{rk}$ disguises the owner's key. The proof also shows the value commitment hides the note's value and the address of the note comes from the same keys, and only the tree check is waived for a note of zero value. An output proof shows the new note's commitment and value commitment are made correctly.

An Orchard Action proves both sides at once, sets the new note's $\rho$ to the spent note's nullifier, and lets a side whose flag is off carry only a dummy note. From NU6.3, when cross-address payments are disabled, the proof also requires the new note to go to the same address as the spent note. The Sprout proof checks the same kinds of facts for two notes in and two out, together with the balance and the tie between the spent notes and the transaction's signing key.

\lsubsection{Encrypting and finding notes}{readableencryption}
\phantomsection\label{decryptivk}\label{decryptovk}\label{saplingandorchardencrypt}\label{saplingandorchardinband}\label{saplinginband}\label{sproutinband}%

The recipient took no part in making the transaction, so the note has to reach the recipient through the block chain. In Sapling and Orchard the sender makes a fresh ephemeral key for every note, combines the ephemeral key with the transmission key of the address to reach a shared secret, and encrypts the note under a key made from the secret. Sprout shares one ephemeral key between the two new notes of a JoinSplit. A second, shorter ciphertext lets the sender's outgoing viewing key read the payment later, and a sender who chose no outgoing viewing key fills the second ciphertext with an encryption under a random key nobody holds.

\textbf{Trial decryption.} The recipient's wallet tries every output on the chain with the incoming viewing key, like trying one key in every locked mailbox, and keeps a note only when the note rebuilt from the decrypted values matches the note commitment on the chain. The rebuilt note shows what arrived but not who sent the note, because the proof does not check the ciphertext and a note carries no sender address. Since Canopy the recipient also uses $\mathit{rseed}$ to check the sender built the note and the ephemeral key correctly \cite{ZIP-212}.

Wallets must use the ephemeral key bytes exactly as the bytes appear on the chain. A wallet may also read transactions still waiting in the mempool, but must treat what the wallet learns as provisional and private, and must not assume the transaction will be mined.

\textbf{Scanning.} To tell whether a received note has been spent, the wallet computes the note's nullifier and watches for the nullifier in later spends. Sapling and Orchard wallets can scan with a full viewing key alone, while Sprout needs the spending key.


\intropart
\vspace{-2ex}
\lsection{Concrete Protocol}{concreteprotocol}

\lsubsection{Integers, Bit Sequences, and Endianness}{endian}

All integers in \Zcash-specific encodings are unsigned, have a fixed
bit length, and are encoded in little-endian byte order \emph{unless otherwise
specified}.

\introlist
The following functions convert between sequences of bits, sequences of bytes,
and integers:

\begin{itemize}
  \item $\ItoLEBSP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat) \times \binaryrange{\ell} \rightarrow \bitseq{\ell}$,
        such that $\ItoLEBSPOf{\ell}{x}$ is the sequence of $\ell$ bits representing $x$ in
        little-endian order;
  \item $\ItoLEOSP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat) \times \binaryrange{\ell} \rightarrow \byteseq{\sceiling{\ell/8}}$,
        such that $\ItoLEOSPOf{\ell}{x}$ is the sequence of $\ceiling{\ell/8}$ bytes representing $x$ in
        little-endian order;
  \item $\ItoBEBSP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat) \times \binaryrange{\ell} \rightarrow \bitseq{\ell}$
        such that $\ItoBEBSPOf{\ell}{x}$ is the sequence of $\ell$ bits representing $x$ in
        big-endian order.
  \item $\LEBStoIP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat) \times \bitseq{\ell} \rightarrow \binaryrange{\ell}$
        such that $\LEBStoIPOf{\ell}{S}$ is the integer represented in little-endian order by the
        \bitSequence $S$ of length $\ell$.
  \item $\LEOStoIP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat \suchthat \ell \bmod 8 = 0) \times \byteseq{\ell/8} \rightarrow \binaryrange{\ell}$
        such that $\LEOStoIPOf{\ell}{S}$ is the integer represented in little-endian order by the
        \byteSequence $S$ of length $\ell/8$.
\notbeforenufive{
  \item $\BEOStoIP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat \suchthat \ell \bmod 8 = 0) \times \byteseq{\ell/8} \rightarrow \binaryrange{\ell}$
        such that $\BEOStoIPOf{\ell}{S}$ is the integer represented in big-endian order by the
        \byteSequence $S$ of length $\ell/8$.
} %notbeforenufive
  \item $\LEBStoOSP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat) \times \bitseq{\ell} \rightarrow \byteseq{\sceiling{\ell/8}}$
        defined as follows: pad the input on the right with $8 \mult \ceiling{\ell/8} - \ell$ zero bits
        so that its length is a multiple of 8 bits. Then convert each group of 8 bits to a byte
        value with the \emph{least} significant bit first, and concatenate the resulting bytes
        in the same order as the groups.
  \item $\LEOStoBSP{} \typecolon (\ell \typecolon \Nat \suchthat \ell \bmod 8 = 0) \times \byteseq{\sceiling{\ell/8}} \rightarrow \bitseq{\ell}$
        defined as follows: convert each byte to a group of 8 bits with the \emph{least} significant
        bit first, and concatenate the resulting groups in the same order as the bytes.
\end{itemize}


\vspace{-2ex}
\extralabel{boxnotation}{\lsubsection{Bit layout diagrams}{bitlayout}}

We sometimes use bit layout diagrams, in which each box of the diagram represents
a sequence of bits. Diagrams are read from left to right, with lines read from
top to bottom; the breaking of boxes across lines has no significance.
The bit length $\ell$ is given explicitly in each box, except when it is obvious
(e.g. for a single bit, or for the notation $\zeros{\ell}$ representing the sequence
of $\ell$ zero bits, or for the output of $\LEBStoOSP{\ell}$).

The entire diagram represents the sequence of \emph{bytes} formed by first
concatenating these \bitSequences, and then treating each subsequence of 8 bits
as a byte with the bits ordered from \emph{most significant} to
\emph{least significant}. Thus the \emph{most significant} bit in each byte
is toward the left of a diagram. \sapling{(This convention is used only in
descriptions of the \Sprout design; in the \SaplingAndOrchard additions,
\bitSequence/\byteSequence conversions are always specified explicitly.)} Where bit fields
are used, the text will clarify their position in each case.


\introsection
\lsubsection{Constants}{constants}

Define:

\begin{formulae}[itemsep=0.2ex]
  \item $\MerkleDepth{Sprout} \typecolon \Nat := 29$
\sapling{
  \item $\MerkleDepth{Sapling} \typecolon \Nat := 32$
} %sapling
\nufive{

} %nufive

\sapling{

} %sapling
\nufive{
  \item $\MerkleHashLength{Orchard} \typecolon \Nat := 255$
} %nufive
  \item $\NOld \typecolon \Nat := 2$
  \item $\NNew \typecolon \Nat := 2$
  \item $\ValueLength \typecolon \Nat := 64$
  \item $\hSigLength \typecolon \Nat := 256$
  \item $\PRFOutputLengthSprout \typecolon \Nat := 256$
\sapling{
  \item $\PRFOutputLengthExpand \typecolon \Nat := 512$
  \item $\PRFOutputLengthNfSapling \typecolon \Nat := 256$
} %sapling
  \item $\NoteCommitRandLengthSprout \typecolon \Nat := 256$
  \item $\RandomSeedLength \typecolon \Nat := 256$
  \item $\AuthPrivateLength \typecolon \Nat := 252$
  \item $\NoteUniquePreRandLength \typecolon \Nat := 252$
\sapling{
  \item $\SpendingKeyLength \typecolon \Nat := 256$
  \item $\DiversifierLength \typecolon \Nat := 88$
\nufive{
  \item $\DiversifierKeyLength \typecolon \Nat := 256$
} %nufive
\nusixthree{
